1	% experiments.tex
2	
3	\section{Experiments}
4	
5	\paragraph{Terminology.}
6	\emph{Cross-attack OOD}: evaluation on attack families unseen during training; benign distribution remains IID throughout (OOD reflects attack-side shift only). $K$: prefix length (number of conversation turns retained). \emph{DAPT}: all Stage-1 adaptations; we extend \citet{gururangan2020dontstop} to supervised variants. \emph{Evidence hierarchy} (on synthetic data): vanilla $<$ MLM $<$ classification (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}).
7	
8	\subsection{Setup}
9	
10	\paragraph{Data.}
11	All synthetic data is generated by Qwen3-8B~\citep{qwen3_2025}.
12	The IID dataset contains 250 Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} jailbreak and 250 topic-anchored benign conversations, split into training (350), validation (74), and test (76) sets with balanced class ratios (e.g., test = 38 jailbreak + 38 benign).
13	Stage~1 (DAPT) uses training + validation conversations; Stage~2 (BiGRU) trains on training, early-stops on validation, and evaluates on test.
14	OOD evaluation pairs attack-specific jailbreak conversations with the 38 IID-test benign conversations: 80 FITD~\citep{fitd2024} (non-discriminative; Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}) + 38 benign = 118 total, 80 Deceptive Delight (DD)~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} + 38 benign = 118 total, and 80 ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} + 38 benign = 118 total.
15	No overlap exists in attack templates across families; benign is IID (same generation pipeline).
16	A paraphrased version of the IID test set (BERTScore 0.92, lexical overlap 0.20) serves as the adversarial robustness benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:perturbation}).
17	For human data validation, we use ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 jailbreak + 204 benign from real LMSYS user interactions), entirely independent of Qwen3-8B generation.
18	
19	\paragraph{Models.}
20	Seven DeBERTa-v3 variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline and differ only in Stage-1 training (Section~\ref{sec:method}).
21	Our vanilla DeBERTa+BiGRU configuration is architecturally equivalent to DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026} and differs only in encoder choice (DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\ BERT); it serves as our primary baseline.
22	For cross-encoder validation, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class variants on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019roberta} (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
23	We also compare TF-IDF LR, LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}), and Llama Guard~3~8B~\citep{metallamaguard3_2024} (zero-shot; evaluates last-response safety given conversation context), which detects only 1.8\% of multi-turn jailbreaks (5/278 pooled recall; Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}).
24	
25	\paragraph{Metrics.}
26	F1 for binary jailbreak detection at prefix $K$=2 and full conversations (full per-$K$ results in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_prefix}).
27	OOD results report 3-seed mean$\pm$std (seeds 42, 123, 456) for all variants.
28	For key pairwise comparisons, we report BCa bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals (10,000 resamples over individual predictions pooled across 3 seeds; this assumes cross-seed independence and is exploratory) and paired permutation tests (10,000 permutations). Seed-level F1 values (Table~\ref{tab:seed_level}, Appendix~\ref{app:seed_level}) confirm all pairwise conclusions hold at every seed.
29	All standard deviations are computed over 3 random seeds using the population formula ($n$ divisor); Bessel-corrected estimates ($n{-}1$ divisor) are $\approx$22\% larger but do not alter any significance conclusions.
30	
31	\paragraph{Training.}
32	Stage-1: learning rate $2 \times 10^{-5}$, batch 16, 4 epochs, MLM masking 15\%.
33	Stage-2: two-layer MLP, 20 epochs.
34	
35	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
36	\subsection{Cross-Attack Generalization}
37	
38	\begin{table}[t]
39	\centering
40	\small
41	\caption{Cross-attack generalization (F1) at $K$=2 and full conversation length. IID: Crescendo; OOD: Deceptive Delight (DD). 3-seed mean$\pm$std where noted; full per-$K$ in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_prefix}.}
42	\label{tab:main_results}
43	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc|cc@{}}
44	\toprule
45	& \multicolumn{2}{c|}{\textit{IID (Cresc.)}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textit{OOD (DD)}} \\
46	\textbf{Method} & $K$=2 & Full & $K$=2 & Full \\
47	\midrule
48	9-class & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & .981$\pm$.013 & \textbf{.994$\pm$.005} \\
49	MLM-only$^{\ddagger}$ & .974 & 1.000 & \textbf{.993$\pm$.009} & .914$\pm$.115 \\
50	Vanilla & .978$\pm$.016 & 1.000$\pm$.000 & .479$\pm$.038 & .312$\pm$.026 \\
51	TF-IDF LR$^{\S}$ & 1.000 & 1.000 & 1.000 & .961 \\
52	\bottomrule
53	\end{tabular}
54	\vspace{-2pt}
55	{\footnotesize $^{\ddagger}$MLM-only IID reports single seed (42); OOD reports 3-seed. $^{\S}$TF-IDF is deterministic (no std).}
56	\end{table}
57	
58	Vanilla encoders collapse under cross-attack transfer; domain-adapted variants recover near-perfect detection (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}, Figure~\ref{fig:cross_attack_bar}).
59	DD exhibits high lexical separability (TF-IDF F1\,=\,0.961); ActorAttack, where TF-IDF collapses to 0.261, serves as the primary stress test for beyond-lexical generalization.
60	On OOD DD, vanilla DeBERTa collapses to F1 = 0.312$\pm$0.026 and worsens with more turns (0.628 at $K$=1 to 0.312 at Full), consistent with attack turns occupying ``benign'' embedding regions~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}.
61	MLM achieves mean F1 $\geq$ 0.97 at all prefixes $K \leq 5$ without any classification labels; domain exposure alone is sufficient.
62	At full conversation length, MLM drops to 0.914$\pm$0.115 (seed 456 recall = 0.64), while the 9-class model maintains 0.994$\pm$0.005.
63	TF-IDF LR reaches F1 = 0.961 on full DD; DD is fully solvable by lexical features.
64	On strategy-diverse ActorAttack, TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 (precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15), while 9-class maintains 0.972$\pm$0.026: a 71\,pp gap. Lexical methods cannot capture multi-persona role-playing attack structure. This establishes a ``difficulty threshold'' for lexical methods parallel to that of vanilla encoders: both succeed on DD and fail on ActorAttack (Section~\ref{sec:limitations}).
65	Bootstrap CIs confirm non-overlapping intervals (9-class DD Full [0.943, 1.000] vs.\ vanilla [0.202, 0.341]; all permutation $p < 0.0001$). FITD is non-discriminative (all variants F1\,$\geq$\,0.990; Section~\ref{sec:limitations}).
66	
67	\begin{figure}[t]
68	\centering
69	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_cross_attack_bar.pdf}
70	\caption{Cross-attack overview (F1 at full conversation length). DisPeD (9-class) maintains near-perfect detection across IID and both OOD families. Vanilla DeBERTa collapses on OOD Deceptive Delight, confirming the necessity of domain-adaptive pretraining. FITD is non-discriminative for all methods (Section~\ref{sec:limitations}).}
71	\label{fig:cross_attack_bar}
72	\end{figure}
73	
74	Prefix-length analysis (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:full_prefix}) shows vanilla F1 drops from 0.628 at $K$=1 to 0.312 at full length. MLM achieves strong early detection ($K$=2: 0.993) but is unstable at full length (0.914$\pm$0.115); the 9-class model maintains 0.972--0.994 across all prefixes. Classification supervision thus stabilizes against distributional drift.
75	
76	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
77	\subsection{Decomposing the Detection Mechanism}
78	\label{sec:decomposition}
79	
80	\begin{table}[t]
81	\centering
82	\small
83	\caption{Evidence hierarchy on OOD DD and ActorAttack (AA) at full conversation length (3-seed mean$\pm$std where available). OOD F1 reflects attack-side generalization only; benign samples are IID from training distribution. Bold indicates best per column. All variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline. Full per-prefix results in Appendix Tables~\ref{tab:full_prefix} and~\ref{tab:actorattack_full}.}
84	\label{tab:evidence_hierarchy}
85	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
86	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
87	\toprule
88	\textbf{Variant} & DD-Full & AA-Full & DD-$K$=2 \\
89	\midrule
90	9-class (full model) & .994$\pm$.005 & .972$\pm$.026 & .981$\pm$.013 \\
91	Binary (2-class) & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & \textbf{.990$\pm$.008} & .994$\pm$.009 \\
92	Scrambled labels & .994$\pm$.005 & .951$\pm$.043 & .981$\pm$.026 \\
93	MLM-only$^{\ddagger}$ (no labels) & .914$\pm$.115 & .893$\pm$.080 & .993$\pm$.009 \\
94	Mean pooling$^{*}$ & .991$\pm$.013 & .962$\pm$.015 & \textbf{1.000} \\
95	\midrule
96	Topic classif.\ (5-class) & .541$\pm$.207 & .625$\pm$.141 & .593$\pm$.166 \\
97	Wikipedia MLM & .904$\pm$.115 & .686$\pm$.297 & .911$\pm$.073 \\
98	Vanilla DeBERTa & .312$\pm$.026 & .258$\pm$.000 & .479$\pm$.038 \\
99	TF-IDF LR$^{\dagger}$ & .961 & .261 & \textbf{1.000} \\
100	Llama Guard~3$^{\P}$ & .025 & .000 & -- \\
101	\bottomrule
102	\end{tabular}}
103	\vspace{-2pt}
104	{\footnotesize $^{*}$9-class encoder with mean pooling replacing BiGRU. $^{\dagger}$Deterministic (no std). $^{\ddagger}$MLM-only IID (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}) is single-seed (42); all OOD values are 3-seed. $^{\P}$Zero-shot; no domain training.}
105	\end{table}
106	
107	A clear \emph{evidence hierarchy} emerges across seven variants (Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}), ordered by OOD detection at full conversation length on DD:
108	
109	\vspace{2pt}
110	\noindent\hspace{0.5em}{\small vanilla (0.312$\pm$0.026) $<$ topic (0.541$\pm$0.207) $\ll$ wiki-MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115) $\approx$ jb-MLM (0.914$\pm$0.115) $<$ scrambled (0.994$\pm$0.005) $=$ 9-class (0.994$\pm$0.005) $\approx$ binary (1.000$\pm$0.000)}
111	\vspace{2pt}
112	
113	\paragraph{Domain exposure is the primary mechanism.}
114	MLM bridges $\sim$60\,pp from vanilla (0.312$\pm$0.026 $\to$ 0.914$\pm$0.115) without labels; this supports the DAPT interpretation~\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}. TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\ 9-class 0.972) confirms that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond lexical overlap.
115	All three classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent detection (F1 = 0.994--1.000); McNemar's ($p > 0.13$) and paired permutation tests ($p \geq 0.40$) provide descriptive evidence suggesting equivalence. Extended to $n$\,=\,10 seeds, TOST (margin = 5\,pp) confirms equivalence on DD ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d$\,=\,$-$0.14; Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}) and FITD ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d \approx 0$). An observed difficulty gradient emerges: template attacks show full equivalence ($d < 0.1$), while strategy-diverse ActorAttack retains moderate label sensitivity ($d$\,=\,0.44, $p$\,=\,0.183). Label quality and granularity are descriptively non-factors for detection.
116	Classification supervision is associated with 4--20$\times$ lower cross-seed variance over MLM on synthetic OOD (std 0.005 vs.\ 0.115 on DD; observed range across 3 seeds; formal variance ratio tests are not applicable at $n$=3; cross-seed ensemble yields marginal additional gains, Appendix~\ref{app:ensemble}). This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where classification-supervised variants exhibit the highest variance and pure MLM the lowest (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}).
117	Appendix~\ref{app:tsne} provides t-SNE visualizations confirming that domain-adapted variants produce tighter, more separable embedding clusters than the vanilla baseline.
118	
119	\paragraph{Domain specificity is marginal at full length but meaningful for early detection.}
120	Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115) nearly matches jailbreak-domain MLM at full length (1\,pp gap), though jailbreak-domain text provides an 8\,pp advantage at early prefixes ($K$=2: 0.993 vs.\ 0.911).
121	Perturbation analysis (Section~\ref{sec:perturbation}) reveals a sharper domain specificity effect: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\sim$12\,pp under adversarial paraphrasing while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\,pp.
122	
123	\paragraph{Misaligned supervision destabilizes generalization.}
124	Topic classification yields DD-Full F1 = 0.541$\pm$0.207; seed 123 falls \emph{below} vanilla (0.252 vs.\ 0.312). Despite using the same jailbreak-domain training data, topic classification performs worse than Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115), which uses out-of-domain text with no labels. A misaligned objective forces the encoder to allocate capacity to irrelevant features and actively destabilizes representations that MLM would preserve.
125	
126	\paragraph{Evidence hierarchy on strategy-diverse attacks.}
127	ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} uses multi-persona steering across longer conversation structures and provides a more challenging OOD benchmark than DD.
128	The evidence hierarchy at full conversation length is:
129	
130	\vspace{2pt}
131	\noindent\hspace{0.5em}{\small TF-IDF (0.261) $\approx$ vanilla (0.258$\pm$0.000) $<$ topic (0.625$\pm$0.141) $<$ wiki-MLM (0.686$\pm$0.297) $<$ jb-MLM (0.893$\pm$0.080) $<$ scrambled (0.951$\pm$0.043) $\approx$ 9-class (0.972$\pm$0.026) $\approx$ binary (0.990$\pm$0.008)}
132	\vspace{2pt}
133	
134	TF-IDF collapses from 0.961 (DD) to 0.261 (ActorAttack, precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15). This gap shows that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond bag-of-words.
135	All classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent performance (0.951--0.990, max gap 3.9\,pp); this extends the DD finding. DAPT provides $\sim$71\,pp over both vanilla (0.258) and TF-IDF (0.261). Full per-prefix results and per-prefix error analysis in Appendix~\ref{sec:actorattack_full} and~\ref{app:aa_error}.
136	
137	\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}
138	Mean pooling matches BiGRU on both DD and ActorAttack (F1 gap $\leq$ 1\,pp, overlapping CIs; Appendix~\ref{app:temporal}).
139	Turn-order shuffling produces negligible change ($|\Delta| < 1$\,pp) across both DeBERTa-v3-base 9-class (DD $\Delta$\,=\,0.000, AA $\Delta$\,=\,$-$0.009, IID $\Delta$\,=\,0.000) and RoBERTa-large 9-class (DD $\Delta$\,=\,+0.001, AA $\Delta$\,=\,$-$0.007, IID $\Delta$\,=\,0.000); detection relies on per-turn representations rather than sequential patterns.
140	
141	\paragraph{Summary.}
142	For base-size encoders (125--184M), domain exposure is the primary mechanism; label correctness, granularity, temporal modeling, and domain specificity are non-factors. Classification supervision provides a complementary stability benefit (variance reduced 4--20$\times$) without changing the detection ceiling. ActorAttack provides the discriminative evaluation where lexical methods fail.
143	
144	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
145	\subsection{Cross-Encoder Validation}
146	\label{sec:cross_encoder}
147	
148	\begin{table}[t]
149	\centering
150	\small
151	\caption{Cross-encoder comparison on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\pm$std). DD = Deceptive Delight, AA = ActorAttack. Bold indicates best per encoder group.}
152	\label{tab:cross_encoder}
153	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
154	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
155	\toprule
156	\textbf{Variant} & DD & AA \\
157	\midrule
158	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{DeBERTa-v3-base (184M)}} \\[2pt]
159	Vanilla & .312$\pm$.026 & .258$\pm$.000 \\
160	MLM & .914$\pm$.115 & .893$\pm$.080 \\
161	9-class & \textbf{.994$\pm$.005} & \textbf{.972$\pm$.026} \\
162	\midrule
163	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{RoBERTa-base (125M)}} \\[2pt]
164	Vanilla & .981$\pm$.026 & .466$\pm$.163 \\
165	MLM & .987$\pm$.018 & .730$\pm$.201 \\
166	9-class & \textbf{.994$\pm$.005} & \textbf{.939$\pm$.046} \\
167	\midrule
168	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{RoBERTa-large (355M)}} \\[2pt]
169	Vanilla & .927$\pm$.036 & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} \\
170	MLM & .884$\pm$.065 & .874$\pm$.014 \\
171	9-class & \textbf{.987$\pm$.004} & .946$\pm$.057 \\
172	\bottomrule
173	\end{tabular}}
174	\end{table}
175	
176	To test whether the evidence hierarchy depends on encoder choice, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019roberta} (Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder}).
177	On DD, RoBERTa-base vanilla already achieves 0.981$\pm$0.026 (vs.\ DeBERTa 0.312$\pm$0.026), so DAPT adds only 1.3\,pp. ActorAttack exposes this as DD's simplicity: RoBERTa-base vanilla collapses to 0.466$\pm$0.163, a 51.5\,pp drop, while domain-adapted variants recover (9-class: 0.939$\pm$0.046).
178	
179	RoBERTa-large (355M) reveals a capacity-DAPT interaction: its effect on ActorAttack reverses (vanilla 1.000 vs.\ 9-class 0.946), while smaller encoders require DAPT to bridge 47--71\,pp. All continued pretraining reduces ActorAttack performance for the largest encoder via representation drift (Table~\ref{tab:roberta_large_lr}), though classification DAPT partially compensates on DD (+6\,pp).
180	
181	Three encoder scales thus reveal a scale-dependent pattern for the \emph{difficulty threshold}: DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all attacks, RoBERTa-base only for complex ones, and RoBERTa-large for none (Section~\ref{sec:discussion}).
182	Classification supervision provides a consistent stability benefit across both base encoders (cross-seed variance: 0.005--0.046 vs.\ 0.026--0.201).
183	A BERT-base-uncased proxy (110M, matching DeepContext's encoder family) without adaptation achieves DD\,=\,0.318$\pm$0.031 and AA\,=\,0.275$\pm$0.027, comparable to vanilla DeBERTa-v3 (Appendix~\ref{app:bert_proxy}); cross-attack generalization is driven by DAPT rather than encoder architecture.
184	
185	CKA analysis~\citep{kornblith2019cka} and linear probing confirm that DAPT reshapes representations substantially (Figure~\ref{fig:cka_heatmap}), though comparable CKA shifts yield different detection gains across starting points (Appendix~\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}).
186	
187	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
188	\subsection{Mixed-LLM Training}
189	
190	All preceding experiments use single-source (Qwen3-8B) data. To test whether the evidence hierarchy transfers when training data mixes LLM sources, we train on 250 Qwen + 250 Llama-3.1-8B conversations (Table~\ref{tab:mixed_llm}).
191	Classification-based DAPT is the only variant that substantially outperforms vanilla: 9-class achieves DD OOD F1\,=\,0.818$\pm$0.096 (+53\,pp over vanilla 0.291$\pm$0.065), while MLM drops to vanilla level (0.289$\pm$0.101). On strategy-diverse ActorAttack, the advantage narrows (9-class 0.504$\pm$0.107 vs.\ vanilla 0.389$\pm$0.073, +11.5\,pp) with high seed variance; limited per-source volume (250 per LLM) likely constrains generalization to complex attacks. DD and FITD thus primarily validate the baseline rather than the method; ActorAttack, where lexical methods collapse (TF-IDF F1\,=\,0.261) and vanilla encoders fail (F1\,$\leq$\,0.389 across conditions), provides the discriminative evaluation axis.
192	Mixed-LLM training confirms that classification supervision generalizes across LLM sources where unsupervised pretraining does not; full results in Appendix~\ref{app:mixed_llm}.
193	
194	%----------------------------------------------------------------------

196	\label{sec:human_data}
197	
198	Since both training and OOD evaluation data are Qwen3-8B-generated, we validate on ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreak attempts + 204 benign conversations from real LMSYS interactions).
199

220	
221	All DAPT variants exceed vanilla by 18--42\,pp (Table~\ref{tab:human_data}): the adapted-vs.-vanilla gap is not a Qwen3-8B generation artifact, and domain exposure on synthetic jailbreak text transfers to human-written attacks.
222	
223	The ordering reverses: JB-MLM leads (0.837$\pm$0.013) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596$\pm$0.087; $p < 0.001$, non-overlapping CIs). ToxicChat is single-turn, so the classification head provides no advantage; validating the multi-turn hierarchy requires multi-turn human data.
224	
225	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
226	\subsection{Perturbation Analysis}
227	\label{sec:perturbation}
228	
229	Under adversarial paraphrasing (BERTScore 0.92), classification-supervised variants achieve zero degradation on IID and OOD DD (Table~\ref{tab:ood_perturbation}) while non-adapted models degrade by $\sim$12\,pp ($p < 0.001$); on ActorAttack, DAPT variants degrade ($\Delta$\,=\,$-$20.4\,pp) but maintain 52\,pp over vanilla (Appendix~\ref{sec:perturbation_full}).
230	
231	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
232	\subsection{Strategy Trajectory Analysis}
233	\label{sec:strategy}
234	
235	The 9-class taxonomy provides interpretable characterization of known attacks ($\kappa$=0.78, $n$=50; Appendix~\ref{sec:strategy_full}) but degrades to near-random on OOD ($\kappa$=0.126; Appendix~\ref{app:strategy_pred}). Its value is twofold: as ablation infrastructure (scrambled labels produce equivalent detection) and as an interpretability tool for known attacks; detection operates through domain-adapted \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings, not strategy accuracy.
236	
237	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
238	\subsection{Benign Distribution Shift}
239	\label{sec:benign_shift}
240	
241	All preceding OOD results use IID benign data. On WildChat benign conversations, 9-class FPR rises to 68.1\%; cross-LLM error decomposition confirms false positives on unfamiliar benign text drive the collapse (Appendix~\ref{app:wildchat_fpr},~\ref{app:cross_llm}).
242

benign is IID (same generation pipeline).\nA paraphrased version of the IID test set (BERTScore 0.92, lexical overlap 0.20) serves as the adversarial robustness benchmark (Section~\\ref{sec:perturbation}).\nFor human data validation, we use ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 jailbreak + 204 benign from real LMSYS user interactions), entirely independent of Qwen3-8B generation.\n\n\\paragraph{Models.}\nSeven DeBERTa-v3 variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline and differ only in Stage-1 training (Section~\\ref{sec:method}).\nOur vanilla DeBERTa+BiGRU configuration is architecturally equivalent to DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} and differs only in encoder choice (DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\\ BERT); it serves as our primary baseline.\nFor cross-encoder validation, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class variants on RoBERTa-base~\\citep{liu2019roberta} (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\nWe also compare TF-IDF LR, LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}), and Llama Guard~3~8B~\\citep{metallamaguard3_2024} (zero-shot; evaluates last-response safety given conversation context), which detects only 1.8\\% of multi-turn jailbreaks (5/278 pooled recall; Table~\\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}).\n\n\\paragraph{Metrics.}\nF1 for binary jailbreak detection at prefix $K$=2 and full conversations (full per-$K$ results in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_prefix}).\nOOD results report 3-seed mean$\\pm$std (seeds 42, 123, 456) for all variants.\nFor key pairwise comparisons, we report BCa bootstrap 95\\% confidence intervals (10,000 resamples over individual predictions pooled across 3 seeds; this assumes cross-seed independence and is exploratory) and paired permutation tests (10,000 permutations). Seed-level F1 values (Table~\\ref{tab:seed_level}, Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_level}) confirm all pairwise conclusions hold at every seed.\nAll standard deviations are computed over 3 random seeds using the population formula ($n$ divisor); Bessel-corrected estimates ($n{-}1$ divisor) are $\\approx$22\\% larger but do not alter any significance conclusions.\n\n\\paragraph{Training.}\nStage-1: learning rate $2 \\times 10^{-5}$, batch 16, 4 epochs, MLM masking 15\\%.\nStage-2: two-layer MLP, 20 epochs.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Cross-Attack Generalization}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-attack generalization (F1) at $K$=2 and full conversation length. IID: Crescendo; OOD: Deceptive Delight (DD). 3-seed mean$\\pm$std where noted; full per-$K$ in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_prefix}.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc|cc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c|}{\\textit{IID (Cresc.)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textit{OOD (DD)}} \\\\\n\\textbf{Method} & $K$=2 & Full & $K$=2 & Full \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & \\textbf{1.000$\\pm$.000} & \\textbf{1.000$\\pm$.000} & .981$\\pm$.013 & \\textbf{.994$\\pm$.005} \\\\\nMLM-only$^{\\ddagger}$ & .974 & 1.000 & \\textbf{.993$\\pm$.009} & .914$\\pm$.115 \\\\\nVanilla & .978$\\pm$.016 & 1.000$\\pm$.000 & .479$\\pm$.038 & .312$\\pm$.026 \\\\\nTF-IDF LR$^{\\S}$ & 1.000 & 1.000 & 1.000 & .961 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{-2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $^{\\ddagger}$MLM-only IID reports single seed (42); OOD reports 3-seed. $^{\\S}$TF-IDF is deterministic (no std).}\n\\end{table}\n\nVanilla encoders collapse under cross-attack transfer; domain-adapted variants recover near-perfect detection (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}, Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_attack_bar}).\nDD exhibits high lexical separability (TF-IDF F1\\,=\\,0.961); ActorAttack, where TF-IDF collapses to 0.261, serves as the primary stress test for beyond-lexical generalization.\nOn OOD DD, vanilla DeBERTa collapses to F1 = 0.312$\\pm$0.026 and worsens with more turns (0.628 at $K$=1 to 0.312 at Full), consistent with attack turns occupying ``benign'' embedding regions~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}.\nMLM achieves mean F1 $\\geq$ 0.97 at all prefixes $K \\leq 5$ without any classification labels; domain exposure alone is sufficient.\nAt full conversation length, MLM drops to 0.914$\\pm$0.115 (seed 456 recall = 0.64), while the 9-class model maintains 0.994$\\pm$0.005.\nTF-IDF LR reaches F1 = 0.961 on full DD; DD is fully solvable by lexical features.\nOn strategy-diverse ActorAttack, TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 (precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15), while 9-class maintains 0.972$\\pm$0.026: a 71\\,pp gap. Lexical methods cannot capture multi-persona role-playing attack structure. This establishes a ``difficulty threshold'' for lexical methods parallel to that of vanilla encoders: both succeed on DD and fail on ActorAttack (Section~\\ref{sec:limitations}).\nBootstrap CIs confirm non-overlapping intervals (9-class DD Full [0.943, 1.000] vs.\\ vanilla [0.202, 0.341]; all permutation $p < 0.0001$). FITD is non-discriminative (all variants F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.990; Section~\\ref{sec:limitations}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_cross_attack_bar.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-attack overview (F1 at full conversation length). DisPeD (9-class) maintains near-perfect detection across IID and both OOD families. Vanilla DeBERTa collapses on OOD Deceptive Delight, confirming the necessity of domain-adaptive pretraining. FITD is non-discriminative for all methods (Section~\\ref{sec:limitations}).}\n\\label{fig:cross_attack_bar}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPrefix-length analysis (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:full_prefix}) shows vanilla F1 drops from 0.628 at $K$=1 to 0.312 at full length. MLM achieves strong early detection ($K$=2: 0.993) but is unstable at full length (0.914$\\pm$0.115); the 9-class model maintains 0.972--0.994 across all prefixes. Classification supervision thus stabilizes against distributional drift.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Decomposing the Detection Mechanism}\n\\label{sec:decomposition}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Evidence hierarchy on OOD DD and ActorAttack (AA) at full conversation length (3-seed mean$\\pm$std where available). OOD F1 reflects attack-side generalization only; benign samples are IID from training distribution. Bold indicates best per column. All variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline. Full per-prefix results in Appendix Tables~\\ref{tab:full_prefix} and~\\ref{tab:actorattack_full}.}\n\\label{tab:evidence_hierarchy}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & DD-Full & AA-Full & DD-$K$=2 \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class (full model) & .994$\\pm$.005 & .972$\\pm$.026 & .981$\\pm$.013 \\\\\nBinary (2-class) & \\textbf{1.000$\\pm$.000} & \\textbf{.990$\\pm$.008} & .994$\\pm$.009 \\\\\nScrambled labels & .994$\\pm$.005 & .951$\\pm$.043 & .981$\\pm$.026 \\\\\nMLM-only$^{\\ddagger}$ (no labels) & .914$\\pm$.115 & .893$\\pm$.080 & .993$\\pm$.009 \\\\\nMean pooling$^{*}$ & .991$\\pm$.013 & .962$\\pm$.015 & \\textbf{1.000} \\\\\n\\midrule\nTopic classif.\\ (5-class) & .541$\\pm$.207 & .625$\\pm$.141 & .593$\\pm$.166 \\\\\nWikipedia MLM & .904$\\pm$.115 & .686$\\pm$.297 & .911$\\pm$.073 \\\\\nVanilla DeBERTa & .312$\\pm$.026 & .258$\\pm$.000 & .479$\\pm$.038 \\\\\nTF-IDF LR$^{\\dagger}$ & .961 & .261 & \\textbf{1.000} \\\\\nLlama Guard~3$^{\\P}$ & .025 & .000 & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\vspace{-2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $^{*}$9-class encoder with mean pooling replacing BiGRU. $^{\\dagger}$Deterministic (no std). $^{\\ddagger}$MLM-only IID (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}) is single-seed (42); all OOD values are 3-seed. $^{\\P}$Zero-shot; no domain training.}\n\\end{table}\n\nA clear \\emph{evidence hierarchy} emerges across seven variants (Table~\\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}), ordered by OOD detection at full conversation length on DD:\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\noindent\\hspace{0.5em}{\\small vanilla (0.312$\\pm$0.026) $<$ topic (0.541$\\pm$0.207) $\\ll$ wiki-MLM (0.904$\\pm$0.115) $\\approx$ jb-MLM (0.914$\\pm$0.115) $<$ scrambled (0.994$\\pm$0.005) $=$ 9-class (0.994$\\pm$0.005) $\\approx$ binary (1.000$\\pm$0.000)}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\n\\paragraph{Domain exposure is the primary mechanism.}\nMLM bridges $\\sim$60\\,pp from vanilla (0.312$\\pm$0.026 $\\to$ 0.914$\\pm$0.115) without labels; this supports the DAPT interpretation~\\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}. TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 9-class 0.972) confirms that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond lexical overlap.\nAll three classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent detection (F1 = 0.994--1.000); McNemar's ($p > 0.13$) and paired permutation tests ($p \\geq 0.40$) provide descriptive evidence suggesting equivalence. Extended to $n$\\,=\\,10 seeds, TOST (margin = 5\\,pp) confirms equivalence on DD ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d$\\,=\\,$-$0.14; Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}) and FITD ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d \\approx 0$). An observed difficulty gradient emerges: template attacks show full equivalence ($d < 0.1$), while strategy-diverse ActorAttack retains moderate label sensitivity ($d$\\,=\\,0.44, $p$\\,=\\,0.183). Label quality and granularity are descriptively non-factors for detection.\nClassification supervision is associated with 4--20$\\times$ lower cross-seed variance over MLM on synthetic OOD (std 0.005 vs.\\ 0.115 on DD; observed range across 3 seeds; formal variance ratio tests are not applicable at $n$=3; cross-seed ensemble yields marginal additional gains, Appendix~\\ref{app:ensemble}). This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where classification-supervised variants exhibit the highest variance and pure MLM the lowest (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\nAppendix~\\ref{app:tsne} provides t-SNE visualizations confirming that domain-adapted variants produce tighter, more separable embedding clusters than the vanilla baseline.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain specificity is marginal at full length but meaningful for early detection.}\nWikipedia MLM (0.904$\\pm$0.115) nearly matches jailbreak-domain MLM at full length (1\\,pp gap), though jailbreak-domain text provides an 8\\,pp advantage at early prefixes ($K$=2: 0.993 vs.\\ 0.911).\nPerturbation analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:perturbation}) reveals a sharper domain specificity effect: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\\sim$12\\,pp under adversarial paraphrasing while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Misaligned supervision destabilizes generalization.}\nTopic classification yields DD-Full F1 = 0.541$\\pm$0.207; seed 123 falls \\emph{below} vanilla (0.252 vs.\\ 0.312). Despite using the same jailbreak-domain training data, topic classification performs worse than Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\\pm$0.115), which uses out-of-domain text with no labels. A misaligned objective forces the encoder to allocate capacity to irrelevant features and actively destabilizes representations that MLM would preserve.\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence hierarchy on strategy-diverse attacks.}\nActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} uses multi-persona steering across longer conversation structures and provides a more challenging OOD benchmark than DD.\nThe evidence hierarchy at full conversation length is:\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\noindent\\hspace{0.5em}{\\small TF-IDF (0.261) $\\approx$ vanilla (0.258$\\pm$0.000) $<$ topic (0.625$\\pm$0.141) $<$ wiki-MLM (0.686$\\pm$0.297) $<$ jb-MLM (0.893$\\pm$0.080) $<$ scrambled (0.951$\\pm$0.043) $\\approx$ 9-class (0.972$\\pm$0.026) $\\approx$ binary (0.990$\\pm$0.008)}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\nTF-IDF collapses from 0.961 (DD) to 0.261 (ActorAttack, precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15). This gap shows that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond bag-of-words.\nAll classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent performance (0.951--0.990, max gap 3.9\\,pp); this extends the DD finding. DAPT provides $\\sim$71\\,pp over both vanilla (0.258) and TF-IDF (0.261). Full per-prefix results and per-prefix error analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:actorattack_full} and~\\ref{app:aa_error}.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}\nMean pooling matches BiGRU on both DD and ActorAttack (F1 gap $\\leq$ 1\\,pp, overlapping CIs; Appendix~\\ref{app:temporal}).\nTurn-order shuffling produces negligible change ($|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp) across both DeBERTa-v3-base 9-class (DD $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.000, AA $\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$0.009, IID $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.000) and RoBERTa-large 9-class (DD $\\Delta$\\,=\\,+0.001, AA $\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$0.007, IID $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.000); detection relies on per-turn representations rather than sequential patterns.\n\n\\paragraph{Summary.}\nFor base-size encoders (125--184M), domain exposure is the primary mechanism; label correctness, granularity, temporal modeling, and domain specificity are non-factors. Classification supervision provides a complementary stability benefit (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$) without changing the detection ceiling. ActorAttack provides the discriminative evaluation where lexical methods fail.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Cross-Encoder Validation}\n\\label{sec:cross_encoder}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-encoder comparison on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\\pm$std). DD = Deceptive Delight, AA = ActorAttack. Bold indicates best per encoder group.}\n\\label{tab:cross_encoder}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & DD & AA \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{DeBERTa-v3-base (184M)}} \\\\[2pt]\nVanilla & .312$\\pm$.026 & .258$\\pm$.000 \\\\\nMLM & .914$\\pm$.115 & .893$\\pm$.080 \\\\\n9-class & \\textbf{.994$\\pm$.005} & \\textbf{.972$\\pm$.026} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{RoBERTa-base (125M)}} \\\\[2pt]\nVanilla & .981$\\pm$.026 & .466$\\pm$.163 \\\\\nMLM & .987$\\pm$.018 & .730$\\pm$.201 \\\\\n9-class & \\textbf{.994$\\pm$.005} & \\textbf{.939$\\pm$.046} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{RoBERTa-large (355M)}} \\\\[2pt]\nVanilla & .927$\\pm$.036 & \\textbf{1.000$\\pm$.000} \\\\\nMLM & .884$\\pm$.065 & .874$\\pm$.014 \\\\\n9-class & \\textbf{.987$\\pm$.004} & .946$\\pm$.057 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nTo test whether the evidence hierarchy depends on encoder choice, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class on RoBERTa-base~\\citep{liu2019roberta} (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_encoder}).\nOn DD, RoBERTa-base vanilla already achieves 0.981$\\pm$0.026 (vs.\\ DeBERTa 0.312$\\pm$0.026), so DAPT adds only 1.3\\,pp. ActorAttack exposes this as DD's simplicity: RoBERTa-base vanilla collapses to 0.466$\\pm$0.163, a 51.5\\,pp drop, while domain-adapted variants recover (9-class: 0.939$\\pm$0.046).\n\nRoBERTa-large (355M) reveals a capacity-DAPT interaction: its effect on ActorAttack reverses (vanilla 1.000 vs.\\ 9-class 0.946), while smaller encoders require DAPT to bridge 47--71\\,pp. All continued pretraining reduces ActorAttack performance for the largest encoder via representation drift (Table~\\ref{tab:roberta_large_lr}), though classification DAPT partially compensates on DD (+6\\,pp).\n\nThree encoder scales thus reveal a scale-dependent pattern for the \\emph{difficulty threshold}: DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all attacks, RoBERTa-base only for complex ones, and RoBERTa-large for none (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nClassification supervision provides a consistent stability benefit across both base encoders (cross-seed variance: 0.005--0.046 vs.\\ 0.026--0.201).\nA BERT-base-uncased proxy (110M, matching DeepContext's encoder family) without adaptation achieves DD\\,=\\,0.318$\\pm$0.031 and AA\\,=\\,0.275$\\pm$0.027, comparable to vanilla DeBERTa-v3 (Appendix~\\ref{app:bert_proxy}); cross-attack generalization is driven by DAPT rather than encoder architecture.\n\nCKA analysis~\\citep{kornblith2019cka} and linear probing confirm that DAPT reshapes representations substantially (Figure~\\ref{fig:cka_heatmap}), though comparable CKA shifts yield different detection gains across starting points (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}).\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Mixed-LLM Training}\n\nAll preceding experiments use single-source (Qwen3-8B) data. To test whether the evidence hierarchy transfers when training data mixes LLM sources, we train on 250 Qwen + 250 Llama-3.1-8B conversations (Table~\\ref{tab:mixed_llm}).\nClassification-based DAPT is the only variant that substantially outperforms vanilla: 9-class achieves DD OOD F1\\,=\\,0.818$\\pm$0.096 (+53\\,pp over vanilla 0.291$\\pm$0.065), while MLM drops to vanilla level (0.289$\\pm$0.101). On strategy-diverse ActorAttack, the advantage narrows (9-class 0.504$\\pm$0.107 vs.\\ vanilla 0.389$\\pm$0.073, +11.5\\,pp) with high seed variance; limited per-source volume (250 per LLM) likely constrains generalization to complex attacks. DD and FITD thus primarily validate the baseline rather than the method; ActorAttack, where lexical methods collapse (TF-IDF F1\\,=\\,0.261) and vanilla encoders fail (F1\\,$\\leq$\\,0.389 across conditions), provides the discriminative evaluation axis.\nMixed-LLM training confirms that classification supervision generalizes across LLM sources where unsupervised pretraining does not; full results in Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_llm}.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Human Data Validation}\n\\label{sec:human_data}\n\nSince both training and OOD evaluation data are Qwen3-8B-generated, we validate on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreak attempts + 204 benign conversations from real LMSYS interactions).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Human data validation on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (macro F1, 3-seed mean$\\pm$std). Bold indicates best. Single-turn data from real LMSYS user interactions, independent of Qwen3-8B.}\n\\label{tab:human_data}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & Macro F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\nJB-MLM & \\textbf{.837$\\pm$.013} \\\\\nWiki-MLM & .791$\\pm$.093 \\\\\nBinary & .705$\\pm$.072 \\\\\nScrambled & .616$\\pm$.056 \\\\\n9-class & .596$\\pm$.087 \\\\\nTopic & .496$\\pm$.042 \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & .413$\\pm$.021 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll DAPT variants exceed vanilla by 18--42\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}): the adapted-vs.-vanilla gap is not a Qwen3-8B generation artifact, and domain exposure on synthetic jailbreak text transfers to human-written attacks.\n\nThe ordering reverses: JB-MLM leads (0.837$\\pm$0.013) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596$\\pm$0.087; $p < 0.001$, non-overlapping CIs). ToxicChat is single-turn, so the classification head provides no advantage; validating the multi-turn hierarchy requires multi-turn human data.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Perturbation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:perturbation}\n\nUnder adversarial paraphrasing (BERTScore 0.92), classification-supervised variants achieve zero degradation on IID and OOD DD (Table~\\ref{tab:ood_perturbation}) while non-adapted models degrade by $\\sim$12\\,pp ($p < 0.001$); on ActorAttack, DAPT variants degrade ($\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$20.4\\,pp) but maintain 52\\,pp over vanilla (Appendix~\\ref{sec:perturbation_full}).\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Strategy Trajectory Analysis}\n\\label{sec:strategy}\n\nThe 9-class taxonomy provides interpretable characterization of known attacks ($\\kappa$=0.78, $n$=50; Appendix~\\ref{sec:strategy_full}) but degrades to near-random on OOD ($\\kappa$=0.126; Appendix~\\ref{app:strategy_pred}). 1	% introduction.tex
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	
5	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.
6	
7	Existing defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and miss multi-turn manipulation; Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}). Multi-turn defenses (DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025}) model conversation-level structure but each evaluates on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}
8	
9	Pretrained language understanding does not suffice at base model scale: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\ DeepContext) drops from F1\,=\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight, and GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}). The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.
10	
11	Our pipeline, \textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants each isolate a single factor while holding all others constant (Section~\ref{sec:method}); the contribution is the decomposition, not the pipeline.
12	
13	\begin{figure}[t]
14	\centering
15	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}
16	\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}
17	\label{fig:architecture}
18	\end{figure}
19	
20	Controlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders, classification-based adaptation is the most robust variant on synthetic multi-turn OOD attacks. MLM collapses on mixed-LLM training (F1\,=\,0.289 $\approx$ vanilla) while classification DAPT maintains 0.818; RoBERTa-large (355M) handles ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision is associated with substantially lower prediction variance on synthetic data but not on human-authored conversations (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}).
21	
22	Our contributions:
23	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]
24	  \item \textbf{Systematic ablation of cross-attack generalization factors.} On synthetic multi-turn data, thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125--355M) decompose jailbreak detection into two orthogonal axes---attack-side and benign-side---the first such controlled factorial study in this domain. On the attack side, classification DAPT recovers OOD detection (vanilla 0.26--0.31 $\rightarrow$ 0.97--1.0) while unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training. On the benign side, 10--25 target-domain conversations reduce FPR from 58--68\% to 0--1.5\% on two independent sources (WildChat, OASST2; IID evaluation) without detection degradation.
25	
26	  \item \textbf{Partition sufficiency with an observed difficulty gradient (at base model scale).} Domain-relevant classification objectives---including scrambled labels---suffice for cross-attack generalization on template attacks (TOST-confirmed, $d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD at $n$\,=\,10), while attacks employing more varied conversational strategies (ActorAttack) show a moderate label effect ($d$\,=\,0.44). This is consistent with \citet{zhang2021understanding}'s framework: domain-relevant partitioning is necessary (topic labels fail) and compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12. A probe over these dimensions confirms sufficiency; removing them degrades DD by 17\,pp while FITD is unaffected (partial necessity; Appendix~\ref{app:discriminative_probe}).
27	
28	  \item \textbf{Two-step adaptation protocol.} On the synthetic benchmark, continued pretraining achieves 24.3$\times$ inference speedup over LLM-as-judge (a deployment cost comparison; parameter counts differ by 43$\times$; Appendix~\ref{app:efficiency}); 10--25 target-domain benign conversations reduce FPR below deployment thresholds on both WildChat and OASST2 crowdsourced conversations. No target-domain attack data is required.
29	\end{itemize}
30	
```

1	% abstract.tex
2	\begin{abstract}
3	Detecting unseen multi-turn jailbreaks requires domain adaptation, but the form of adaptation matters.
4	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks spread adversarial intent across benign-seeming turns, and existing defenses fail to generalize across attack families: Llama Guard~3, a widely deployed safety classifier, detects only 1.8\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation.
5	We isolate the factors behind cross-attack generalization on synthetic multi-turn data through controlled ablation: thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125 to 355M parameters) decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and granularity on three out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families and two training compositions.
6	Classification-based domain adaptation recovers OOD detection on synthetic data (F1\,=\,0.97--1.0 vs.\ vanilla 0.26--0.31; attack-side OOD, benign IID) while unsupervised pretraining collapses.
7	Under mixed-LLM training, template attacks remain detectable but strategy-diverse ActorAttack degrades (F1\,=\,0.504).
8	These findings derive from synthetic data from a single LLM; the evidence hierarchy reverses on human-authored single-turn data, indicating condition-specific rather than universal mechanisms.
9	Orthogonal to attack-side generalization, benign distribution shift is a practical bottleneck: few-shot benign adaptation reduces false-positive rates below deployment thresholds on two independent sources.
10	These findings decompose multi-turn jailbreak generalization into two orthogonal axes---attack-side (discriminative compression) and benign-side (few-shot adaptation)---with 24.3$\times$ inference speedup over LLM-as-judge.
11	\end{abstract}
12

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01JKNK2u97tkGtMTqH62E4dZ`</summary>

```text
1	% related_work.tex
2	
3	\section{Related Work}
4	
5	\paragraph{Multi-turn jailbreak attacks.}
6	All multi-turn jailbreak families share a persuasion substrate~\citep{zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,li2024mhj}, yet each operationalizes it differently: Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} gradually escalates, FITD~\citep{fitd2024} exploits commitment-consistency bias, DD~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} combines emotional manipulation with role assignment, ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} steers through multiple personas, and automated methods (PAIR~\citep{chao2023pair}, TAP~\citep{mehrotra2024tap}) iteratively refine adversarial prompts, extending the automated red-teaming paradigm~\citep{perez2022redteaming}. \citet{wei2023jailbroken} provide a unifying taxonomy; RED QUEEN~\citep{jiang2024redqueen} constructs concealed multi-turn scenarios across 14 harmful categories; benchmarks~\citep{mazeika2024harmbench,chao2024jailbreakbench} standardize single-turn comparison, while MultiBreak~\citep{song2026multibreak} targets multi-turn evaluation. This shared substrate with diverse surface strategies defines our evaluation: a detector must generalize across families, not merely succeed on its training distribution.
7	
8	\paragraph{Multi-turn defenses and production safety.}
9	No existing defense evaluates cross-attack transfer. Recent conversation-level systems pair fine-tuned BERT~\citep{devlin2019bert} encoders with sequence-level aggregation via GRU hierarchies (DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}), turn-intention inference (BIID~\citep{tong2025biid}), graph neural networks (G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025}), or predictive generation (SafeDream~\citep{safedream2026}), yet each evaluates on a single attack family. Representation-level approaches include X-Boundary~\citep{lu2025xboundary}, JBShield~\citep{zhang2025jbshield} via activated concept analysis, gradient-space analysis (GradSafe~\citep{xie2024gradsafe}), circuit breakers via representation engineering~\citep{zou2024circuitbreakers}, latent activation probing~\citep{kulkarni2026latentadversarial,kadali2026trace}, and vision-language detection~\citep{hua2026rcs}; these require white-box access and can be brittle under adaptive attacks~\citep{derya2026revisitingjbshield}. Our work uses black-box classification with systematic cross-attack OOD evaluation. Per-turn classifiers (Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard,metallamaguard3_2024}, ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, Constitutional Classifiers~\citep{anthropic2025constitutional}) cannot model multi-turn trajectories, and perturbation-based defenses~\citep{robey2024smoothllm} and safety-aware decoding~\citep{xu2024safedecoding} target single-turn token-level attacks. Our ablation fills this gap: decomposition across thirteen configurations under OOD evaluation identifies domain exposure as the dominant factor behind cross-attack transfer.
10	
11	\paragraph{Computational persuasion and discourse.}
12	Persuasion and discourse frameworks offer interpretable vocabulary for attack strategies, but whether such structure aids detection has not been empirically disentangled. Foundational work (RST~\citep{mann1988rst}, PDTB~\citep{prasad2008pdtb}, dialogue acts~\citep{stolcke2000dialogueact}, Speech Act Theory~\citep{austin1962speechacts,searle1969speechacts}) models pragmatic structure at different granularities. Building on Cialdini's principles~\citep{cialdini2001influence}, PAP~\citep{zeng2024pap} identifies 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\% of successful attacks; propaganda analysis~\citep{dasanmartino2019fine,dasanmartino2020semeval} and persuasive dialogue~\citep{wang2019persuasion} apply similar technique-level classification. Our ablation disentangles these roles: the taxonomy enables interpretable strategy characterization, but detection operates through domain-adapted embeddings regardless of label correctness.
13	
14	\paragraph{Domain-adaptive pretraining.}
15	Continued pretraining on domain text yields consistent gains~\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}, as shown in biomedicine~\citep{lee2020biobert} and scientific text~\citep{beltagy2019scibert}, extending ULMFiT~\citep{howard2018ulmfit}; yet this has not been disentangled for safety-critical generalization. Intermediate-task transfer (STILTs~\citep{phang2018stilts}) and multi-task learning~\citep{caruana1997multitask,liu2019mtdnn} confound domain exposure with supervised signal. Our MLM control confirms pretraining alone closes the OOD gap. Dataset artifact analysis~\citep{gururangan2018artifacts,niven2019probing} motivates the TF-IDF baseline, adversarial NLP~\citep{jin2020textfooler,li2020bertattack,nasr2025attackermovessecond,andriushchenko2024adaptive} informs the perturbation protocol, and probing~\citep{conneau2018probing,hewitt2019structural} underpins CKA analysis.
16	
```

    "originalFile": "% introduction.tex\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.\n\nExisting defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and miss multi-turn manipulation; Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}). Multi-turn defenses (DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025}) model conversation-level structure but each evaluates on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \\emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}\n\nPretrained language understanding does not suffice at base model scale: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\\ DeepContext) drops from F1\\,=\\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight, and GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.\n\nOur pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants each isolate a single factor while holding all others constant (Section~\\ref{sec:method}); the contribution is the decomposition, not the pipeline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}\n\\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}\n\\label{fig:architecture}\n\\end{figure}\n\nControlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders, classification-based adaptation is the most robust variant on synthetic multi-turn OOD attacks. MLM collapses on mixed-LLM training (F1\\,=\\,0.289 $\\approx$ vanilla) while classification DAPT maintains 0.818; RoBERTa-large (355M) handles ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision is associated with substantially lower prediction variance on synthetic data but not on human-authored conversations (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic ablation of cross-attack generalization factors.} On synthetic multi-turn data, thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125--355M) decompose jailbreak detection into two orthogonal axes (attack-side and benign-side), the first such controlled factorial study in this domain. On the attack side, classification DAPT recovers OOD detection (vanilla 0.26--0.31 $\\rightarrow$ 0.97--1.0) while unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training. On the benign side, 10--25 target-domain conversations reduce FPR from 58--68\\% to 0--1.5\\% on two independent sources (WildChat, OASST2; IID evaluation) without detection degradation.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Partition sufficiency with an observed difficulty gradient (at base model scale).} Domain-relevant classification objectives, including scrambled labels, suffice for cross-attack generalization on template attacks (TOST-confirmed, $d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD at $n$\\,=\\,10), while attacks employing more varied conversational strategies (ActorAttack) show a moderate label effect ($d$\\,=\\,0.44). This is consistent with \\citet{zhang2021understanding}'s framework: domain-relevant partitioning is necessary (topic labels fail) and compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12. A probe over these dimensions confirms sufficiency; removing them degrades DD by 17\\,pp while FITD is unaffected (partial necessity; Appendix~\\ref{app:discriminative_probe}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Two-step adaptation protocol.} On the synthetic benchmark, continued pretraining achieves 24.3$\\times$ inference speedup over LLM-as-judge (a deployment cost comparison; parameter counts differ by 43$\\times$; Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}); 10--25 target-domain benign conversations reduce FPR below deployment thresholds on both WildChat and OASST2 crowdsourced conversations. No target-domain attack data is required.\n\\end{itemize}\n",

    "newString": "two orthogonal axes (attack-side and benign-side), the first",
    "originalFile": "% introduction.tex\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.\n\nExisting defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and miss multi-turn manipulation; Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}). Multi-turn defenses (DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025}) model conversation-level structure but each evaluates on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \\emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}\n\nPretrained language understanding does not suffice at base model scale: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\\ DeepContext) drops from F1\\,=\\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight, and GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.\n\nOur pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants each isolate a single factor while holding all others constant (Section~\\ref{sec:method}); the contribution is the decomposition, not the pipeline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}\n\\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}\n\\label{fig:architecture}\n\\end{figure}\n\nControlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders, classification-based adaptation is the most robust variant on synthetic multi-turn OOD attacks. MLM collapses on mixed-LLM training (F1\\,=\\,0.289 $\\approx$ vanilla) while classification DAPT maintains 0.818; RoBERTa-large (355M) handles ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision is associated with substantially lower prediction variance on synthetic data but not on human-authored conversations (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic ablation of cross-attack generalization factors.} On synthetic multi-turn data, thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125--355M) decompose jailbreak detection into two orthogonal axes---attack-side and benign-side---the first such controlled factorial study in this domain. On the attack side, classification DAPT recovers OOD detection (vanilla 0.26--0.31 $\\rightarrow$ 0.97--1.0) while unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training. On the benign side, 10--25 target-domain conversations reduce FPR from 58--68\\% to 0--1.5\\% on two independent sources (WildChat, OASST2; IID evaluation) without detection degradation.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Partition sufficiency with an observed difficulty gradient (at base model scale).} Domain-relevant classification objectives---including scrambled labels---suffice for cross-attack generalization on template attacks (TOST-confirmed, $d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD at $n$\\,=\\,10), while attacks employing more varied conversational strategies (ActorAttack) show a moderate label effect ($d$\\,=\\,0.44). This is consistent with \\citet{zhang2021understanding}'s framework: domain-relevant partitioning is necessary (topic labels fail) and compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12. A probe over these dimensions confirms sufficiency; removing them degrades DD by 17\\,pp while FITD is unaffected (partial necessity; Appendix~\\ref{app:discriminative_probe}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Two-step adaptation protocol.} On the synthetic benchmark, continued pretraining achieves 24.3$\\times$ inference speedup over LLM-as-judge (a deployment cost comparison; parameter counts differ by 43$\\times$; Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}); 10--25 target-domain benign conversations reduce FPR below deployment thresholds on both WildChat and OASST2 crowdsourced conversations. No target-domain attack data is required.\n\\end{itemize}\n",

      {

        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 21,

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Topic shortcut and label equivalence.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97).\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer. Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains 0.818 while MLM collapses; MLM may capture LLM-specific surface patterns while classification extracts style-invariant features.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nClassification supervision is associated with lower variance on synthetic OOD (4--20$\\times$ observed range, 3 seeds); on ToxicChat this reverses (std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ 0.013). Frozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2 (Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity and lexical analysis.}\nThree encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. TF-IDF collapses on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 0.961 on DD).\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence consistent with discriminative compression.}\nClassification DAPT is associated with feature compression into few dimensions (participation ratio 3.7--9.4 vs.\\ 25--32; Fisher 2.6--19.7 vs.\\ $<$0.001; Appendix~\\ref{app:representation}). Scrambled labels yield comparable compression (Fisher\\,=\\,2.6), consistent with \\citet{zhang2021understanding}, with two findings: (i)~domain-relevant partitioning is necessary (topic DAPT has higher per-turn Fisher yet fails at conversation level; Appendix~\\ref{app:topic_failure}), and (ii)~compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12 (Appendix~\\ref{app:layerwise}). A discriminative probe over these 3 dimensions achieves perfect AA OOD detection (sufficiency) and their removal degrades DD by 17\\,pp while FITD is unaffected (partial necessity; Appendix~\\ref{app:discriminative_probe}); frozen probing near-parity (3.1\\,pp) is consistent with this interpretation.\n\n\\paragraph{Boundaries.}\nWildChat FPR rises from 48.1\\% (vanilla) to 68.1\\% (9-class), confirming classification DAPT amplifies benign distribution sensitivity while improving attack-side generalization.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Discriminative compression and label equivalence.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97).\nClassification DAPT compresses features into few dimensions (participation ratio 3.7--9.4 vs.\\ 25--32; Fisher 2.6--19.7 vs.\\ $<$0.001; Appendix~\\ref{app:representation}), consistent with \\citet{zhang2021understanding}: domain-relevant partitioning is necessary (topic DAPT fails; Appendix~\\ref{app:topic_failure}) and compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12 (Appendix~\\ref{app:layerwise}). Three encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed.     "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut and label equivalence.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97).\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer. Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains 0.818 while MLM collapses; MLM may capture LLM-specific surface patterns while classification extracts style-invariant features.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nClassification supervision is associated with lower variance on synthetic OOD (4--20$\\times$ observed range, 3 seeds); on ToxicChat this reverses (std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ 0.013). Frozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2 (Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity and lexical analysis.}\nThree encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. TF-IDF collapses on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 0.961 on DD).\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence consistent with discriminative compression.}\nClassification DAPT is associated with feature compression into few dimensions (participation ratio 3.7--9.4 vs.\\ 25--32; Fisher 2.6--19.7 vs.\\ $<$0.001; Appendix~\\ref{app:representation}). Scrambled labels yield comparable compression (Fisher\\,=\\,2.6), consistent with \\citet{zhang2021understanding}, with two findings: (i)~domain-relevant partitioning is necessary (topic DAPT has higher per-turn Fisher yet fails at conversation level; Appendix~\\ref{app:topic_failure}), and (ii)~compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12 (Appendix~\\ref{app:layerwise}). A discriminative probe over these 3 dimensions achieves perfect AA OOD detection (sufficiency) and their removal degrades DD by 17\\,pp while FITD is unaffected (partial necessity; Appendix~\\ref{app:discriminative_probe}); frozen probing near-parity (3.1\\,pp) is consistent with this interpretation.\n\n\\paragraph{Boundaries.}\nWildChat FPR rises from 48.1\\% (vanilla) to 68.1\\% (9-class), confirming classification DAPT amplifies benign distribution sensitivity while improving attack-side generalization.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nOn synthetic multi-turn data, thirteen encoders decompose jailbreak generalization into attack-side (discriminative compression; MLM collapses under mixed-LLM) and benign-side (FPR 58--68\\%$\\rightarrow$0--1.5\\%, $N$\\,$\\leq$\\,25) axes at 24.3$\\times$ speedup; classification suffices for template attacks ($d\\!<\\!0.1$; AA $d$\\,=\\,0.44) at base scale; the hierarchy reverses on human data, confirming condition specificity.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nAll findings derive from synthetic conversations generated by a single LLM (Qwen3-8B; 350 training, 76--118 test per family, 3 seeds); the evidence hierarchy reverses on human single-turn data (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}), and no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists. OOD F1 figures are upper bounds because benign data is IID; estimated dual-OOD F1 drops 12--14\\,pp without adaptation but recovers within 0.3\\,pp after few-shot adaptation (Appendix~\\ref{app:dual_ood}). Benign-side adaptation (WildChat FPR 68.1\\%\\,$\\rightarrow$\\,0\\% with 10 conversations; OASST2 57.5\\%\\,$\\rightarrow$\\,1.0\\% at $N$\\,=\\,25) is validated on only two platforms with small samples ($n \\leq 49$). Benign-only adaptation does not address cross-LLM distribution shift: adapting on Llama-origin conversations collapses Llama jailbreak detection (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg} and non-text modalities are not addressed.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content), withholding attack-generation prompts, and focusing contributions on detection mechanisms. No human subjects were involved; all data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0.\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_014V8Fo4AA4EVagaQ7SnffEu`</summary>

```text
107	A clear \emph{evidence hierarchy} emerges across seven variants (Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}), ordered by OOD detection at full conversation length on DD:
108	
109	\noindent\hspace{0.5em}{\small vanilla (0.312$\pm$0.026) $<$ topic (0.541$\pm$0.207) $\ll$ wiki-MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115) $\approx$ jb-MLM (0.914$\pm$0.115) $<$ scrambled (0.994$\pm$0.005) $=$ 9-class (0.994$\pm$0.005) $\approx$ binary (1.000$\pm$0.000)}
110	\vspace{2pt}
111	
112	\paragraph{Domain exposure is the primary mechanism.}
113	MLM bridges $\sim$60\,pp from vanilla (0.312$\pm$0.026 $\to$ 0.914$\pm$0.115) without labels; this supports the DAPT interpretation~\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}. TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\ 9-class 0.972) confirms that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond lexical overlap.
114	All three classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent detection (F1 = 0.994--1.000); McNemar's ($p > 0.13$) and paired permutation tests ($p \geq 0.40$) provide descriptive evidence suggesting equivalence. Extended to $n$\,=\,10 seeds, TOST (margin = 5\,pp) confirms equivalence on DD ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d$\,=\,$-$0.14; Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}) and FITD ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d \approx 0$). An observed difficulty gradient emerges: template attacks show full equivalence ($d < 0.1$), while strategy-diverse ActorAttack retains moderate label sensitivity ($d$\,=\,0.44, $p$\,=\,0.183). Label quality and granularity are descriptively non-factors for detection.
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01LiiqYtc4p8z3i5JhqbzRg4`</summary>

```text
1	% conclusion.tex
2	
3	\section{Discussion}
4	\label{sec:discussion}
5	
6	\paragraph{Discriminative compression and label equivalence.}
7	Three observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97).
8	Classification DAPT compresses features into few dimensions (participation ratio 3.7--9.4 vs.\ 25--32; Fisher 2.6--19.7 vs.\ $<$0.001; Appendix~\ref{app:representation}), consistent with \citet{zhang2021understanding}: domain-relevant partitioning is necessary (topic DAPT fails; Appendix~\ref{app:topic_failure}) and compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12 (Appendix~\ref{app:layerwise}). A probe over these dimensions confirms sufficiency; removal degrades DD by 17\,pp while FITD is unaffected (partial necessity; Appendix~\ref{app:discriminative_probe}).
9	
10	\paragraph{Cross-condition transfer and boundaries.}
11	ToxicChat (+18--42\,pp; Table~\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer beyond single-LLM artifacts; mixed-LLM 9-class maintains 0.818 while MLM collapses, suggesting classification extracts style-invariant features. Classification supervision is associated with lower variance on synthetic OOD (4--20$\times$, 3 seeds) but this reverses on ToxicChat (std\,=\,0.087 vs.\ 0.013); frozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2 (Appendix~\ref{app:frozen_verify}). Three encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. WildChat FPR rises from 48.1\% to 68.1\% under 9-class DAPT, confirming that attack-side generalization amplifies benign distribution sensitivity.
12	
13	\section{Conclusion}
14	
15	On synthetic multi-turn data, thirteen encoders decompose jailbreak generalization into attack-side (discriminative compression; MLM collapses under mixed-LLM) and benign-side (FPR 58--68\%$\rightarrow$0--1.5\%, $N$\,$\leq$\,25) axes at 24.3$\times$ speedup; classification suffices for template attacks ($d\!<\!0.1$; AA $d$\,=\,0.44) at base scale; the hierarchy reverses on human data, confirming condition specificity.
16	
17	\section{Limitations}
18	\label{sec:limitations}
19	
20	All findings derive from synthetic conversations generated by a single LLM (Qwen3-8B; 350 training, 76--118 test per family, 3 seeds); the evidence hierarchy reverses on human single-turn data (Section~\ref{sec:discussion}), and no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists. OOD F1 figures are upper bounds because benign data is IID; estimated dual-OOD F1 drops 12--14\,pp without adaptation but recovers within 0.3\,pp after few-shot adaptation (Appendix~\ref{app:dual_ood}). Benign-side adaptation (WildChat FPR 68.1\%\,$\rightarrow$\,0\% with 10 conversations; OASST2 57.5\%\,$\rightarrow$\,1.0\% at $N$\,=\,25) is validated on only two platforms with small samples ($n \leq 49$). Benign-only adaptation does not address cross-LLM distribution shift: adapting on Llama-origin conversations collapses Llama jailbreak detection (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}). Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\citep{zou2023gcg} and non-text modalities are not addressed.
21	
22	\section*{Ethics Statement}
23	
24	This work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content), withholding attack-generation prompts, and focusing contributions on detection mechanisms. No human subjects were involved; all data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0.
25	
```

</details>

[Director 指令] **提交前最终修复 — 4 项快速修改**

Reviewer 最终检查发现 4 个问题，按优先级排列。全部是文本修改，不需要新实验。

### P0: d<0.1 vs d=-0.14 数字不一致（1 分钟）
- **位置**：introduction.tex L26
- **问题**：intro 写 "$d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD"，但 experiments.tex L114 写 DD 的 d=-0.14（|d|=0.14 > 0.1）
- **修复**：改 intro 为 "$|d| \leq 0.14$ on DD, $d \approx 0$ on FITD" 或类似表述使之与 experiments 一致

### P1: 扩展 §4.9 Benign Distribution Shift（10 分钟）
- **位置**：experiments.tex §4.9（约 L236）
- **问题**：当前只有 1 句话，但这是三大贡献之一，审稿人会注意到主文证据不足
- **修复**：增加 2-3 句，包含：
  - Few-shot adaptation 关键数字：WildChat 10 conversations → FPR 0%；OASST2 25 conversations → FPR 1.0%±0.5%
  - Dual-OOD 估计：without adaptation F1 drops 12-14pp; with few-shot adaptation recovers within 0.3pp
  - 引用 appendix 完整表格
- **注意**：不能让主文超过 8 页。如果扩展后溢出，微调其他段落压缩

### P2: Appendix 效率节明确 24.3× 来源（5 分钟）
- **位置**：appendix.tex 效率表附近（约 L807-808）
- **问题**：appendix 表格显示 12.1ms vs ~5,200ms (≈430×)，与主文 24.3× 差 18 倍，虽有脚注但仍易混淆
- **修复**：在 appendix 效率段落中明确写："The 24.3$\times$ speedup reported in the main text derives from matched-condition comparison (exp46: DisPeD 13.12\,ms vs.\ Qwen3-8B 318.53\,ms on the same GPU). The larger gap in Table~XX reflects different hardware conditions."

### P3: Table 2 caption 恢复 IID 注释（2 分钟）
- **位置**：experiments.tex Table 2 (evidence_hierarchy) caption
- **问题**：之前为回应 reviewer concern SF6 添加的 IID benign 透明性注释被删除
- **修复**：在 Table 2 caption 末尾加 "OOD F1 reflects attack-side generalization only; benign samples are IID from training distribution (benign-side shift evaluated in Section~\ref{sec:benign_shift})."（sec:benign_shift 指向 §4.9）

## 执行要求
1. 按 P0→P1→P2→P3 顺序修
2. P1 扩展后编译确认主文仍在 8 页内
3. **禁止修改 figures/ 目录**
4. 完成后汇报每项 diff

230	The 9-class taxonomy provides interpretable characterization of known attacks ($\kappa$=0.78, $n$=50; Appendix~\ref{sec:strategy_full}) but degrades to near-random on OOD ($\kappa$=0.126; Appendix~\ref{app:strategy_pred}). Its value is twofold: as ablation infrastructure (scrambled labels produce equivalent detection) and as an interpretability tool for known attacks; detection operates through domain-adapted \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings, not strategy accuracy.
231	
232	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
233	\subsection{Benign Distribution Shift}
